

The Golden Lineage
*Homage to Buddha, Dharma, and Sangha!*

Of all the possible times and places in which sentient life has arisen, we are blessed to live in a moment when the human beings of Jambudvīpa still have an opportunity to make direct contact with the Triple Gem; the teachings of the Tathāgata continue to be upheld by the Ārya Sangha, despite the events of our time that reveal so clearly the infallibility of the laws of impermanence and interdependence. In light of *sarva-d**uḥkha**m,* or universal suffering, the history of Tibet offers an illustration of how rare and precious our Dharma treasure truly is; for had the great eighth-century masters known as the Khenpo, the Lopön and the Chögyal (Khen Lob Chö Sum) not recalled their past lives and kept their vow to bring Dharma to the Snow Land, Tibetan Buddhism as we know it today would not exist.

*Transmission and Realization*
Dharma is a twofold treasure: it consists of the teachings of liberation, the āgama, known in Tibet as *lung*; and the fruit of realization, *adhigama* or *togpa* (*rtog pa*). The precious scriptures of the transmitted teachings present the path to realization created for the world by the Buddha Śākyamuni, the Fourth Tathāgata of our Fortunate Era.
Among the scholars of the Buddhist schools, many dates for the Buddha’s Parinirvāṇa have been calculated. The Pāli Canon, following the Dotted Record of the Arhat Upāli, set the date of the Parinirvāṇa at 544 BCE, a position upheld to the present day by the Theravādins; but many Tibetan scholars favor far earlier dates, including Chomden Rigrel’s disciple Upa Losel (2146 BCE), Atiśa Dipaṃkāra (2136 BCE), and Sakya Paṇḍita (2133 BCE). The sixteenth-century scholar Pakpa Lhundrup proposed the date of 961 BCE, while Gö Lotsāwa, author of the *Blue Annals,* following a chronology accepted in China and Japan, placed the date at 949 BCE. Butön Rinchen Drub, studying the Kālacakra, calculated the Parinirvāṇa to have occurred in 876 BCE. The paṇḍita Kamalaśīla, disciple of Abbot Śāntarakṣita, proposed the date of 718 BCE, while the thirteenth-century master Orgyenpa Rinchen Pel considered 651 BCE to be correct. Modern Western scholarship has proposed dates ranging from 483 BCE to 383 BCE, although the recent discovery of an ancient Buddhist shrine at Lumbinī has produced a new reckoning by some, who set the Parinirvāṇa in the sixth century BCE. It is our great good fortune that the span of years separating us from the Fourth Buddha has not yet caused his teachings to disappear, for his words have been diligently compiled over the centuries by those dedicated to the Dharma’s survival.
These words took many forms, for the Tathāgata presented his teachings in twelve distinct *Aṅgas,* or Aspects of Excellent Speech, the *Dvādaśāṅgapravacana* (*gsung rab yan lag bcu gnyis*). They include the Sūtras or discourses (*sūtra*; *mdo’i sde*); poetic summaries (*geya*; *dbyangs kyis bsnyad pa sde*); prophecies (*vyākaraṇa*; *lung bstan pa’i sde*); discourses in verse (*gāthā*; *tshigs su bcad pa’i sde*); intentional statements (*udāna*; *ched du brjod pa’i sde*); contextual accounts (*nidāna*; *gleng gzhi’i sde*); testimonies of realization (*avadāna*; *rtogs pa brjod pa’i sde*); historical explanations (*itivṛttaka*; *de lta bu byung ba’i sde*); accounts of former lives (*jātaka*; *skyes pa’i rabs kyi sde*); detailed explanations (*vaipulya*; *shin tu rgyas pa*); wondrous discourses (*abidhutadharma*; *rmad du byung ba’i chos kyi sde*); and definitive explanations (*upadeśa*; *gtan la phab pa’i sde*).
The *Aṅgas* are traditionally gathered into three sections or Turnings of the Wheel of Dharma. In Tibetan, they are known as the *Kadangpo (bka’ dang po),* the First Turning that discloses the Four Noble Truths (bka’ dang po bden bzhi’i chos ‘khor); the *Kaparpa (bka’ bar pa),* the Second Turning that reveals the absence of characteristics (bka’ bar pa mtshan nyid med pa’i chos ‘khor); and the *Katama (bka’ tha ma),* the Third Turning of the final teachings (bka’ tha ma legs pa rnam par phye ba’i chos ‘khor). In the First Turning, the Buddha taught the Four Noble Truths of suffering, cause, liberation, and the path, and expounded the teachings upheld by the Śrāvakas or Hearers of the Doctrine. These include the collections traditionally known as the Tripiṭaka, or Three Baskets: the basket of Discipline, (*vinaya; ’dul ba*) which presented guidelines for conduct for the Sangha; the basket of Discourses (*sūtra; mdo*), which held the scriptures that are traditionally referred to as the Āgamas*;* and the systematic study of reality known as the Abhidharma (*mngon rtogs*), compiled in ancient times in the *maṭṛka* or ‘matrices,’ and gradually elaborated by great paṇḍitas in the positions of the early philosophical schools, notably the Vaibāṣika and Sautrāntika. In the Second Turning, the Buddha brought forth the teachings of śūnyatā, often translated into English as ‘emptiness’, as expressed in the Prajñāpāramitā, the Mother of Buddhas. Śūnyatā and its implications were examined with meticulous care in the philosophical tradition sometimes called the Outer Madhyamaka, expressed in the doctrines of the Prasaṅgikas and Svātantrikas. In the Third Turning are found the teachings of Buddha-nature, Tathāgatagarbha, that are examined in higher forms of Cittamātra-Yogācāra, and what is known in the Nyingma tradition as the Great Madhyamaka, which unites the teachings of Nāgārjuna and Asaṅga. Further, beyond the limits of the teachings of cause and effect, vast and deep as the ocean, the Mantrayāna was transmitted by the Jīna to retinues of Buddhas, Bodhisattvas, gods, nāgās, yakṣas and ḍākiṇīs. These teachings included the Unsurpassed Tantras of the father, mother, and nondual lineages and the sublime Inner Yogas of the Nyingma. In this way did the essence of the Buddha’s realization emerge as transmitted teachings that suited the varying capacities of the sentient beings of the Six Realms.
The liberatory power of the Buddhadharma can be understood in terms of its Ground (*gzhi*), Path (*lam*), and Fruit (*’bras bu*). According to our teachers and guides, the Ground or basis of the Dharma is that all beings have the potential for enlightenment: our true nature is inseparable from Buddhahood. The Path is the way offered by the teachings to reveal the truth of the Ground and elicit a transformation of the mind; it clears up the karmic, emotional, cognitive, and habitual obscurations of our intrinsic enlightened nature–*karma* and *kleśa*–by presenting the view (tawa, *lta ba*), guiding the practice (gompa, *sgom pa*), and nurturing the conduct (shöpa, *spyod pa*) of full understanding. View, practice, and conduct were explored to their fullest in the sublime commentaries of the great paṇḍitas; their analyses became the basis for the emergence of the Indian philosophical schools. Tibet, receiving these traditions, in turn developed numerous schools, each with its own distinctive expressions and interpretations of tawa, gompa, and shöpa. In time, five branches of philosophical inquiry emerged in the Snow Land: Pramāṇa (tshad ma), or ‘valid cognition’; the teachings of the Madhyamikas; Abhidharma; the Vinaya teachings; and Prajñāpāramitā.
The Fruit of Buddhadharma is the perfect realization of the ground, in which Buddhahood is fully achieved in the Dharma of Realization. On the path, realization of the ground is achieved through practice, in Three Trainings that lead to liberation: the *triśikṣa* or *lhagpé labpa sum* (*lhag pa’i bslab pa gsum*) of discipline (śīla; tshul khrims), meditative absorption (*samādhi; ting nge ’dzin*), and wisdom (*prajñā; shes rab*). Traditionally, each training is associated with a different part of the Lung, or transmitted teachings: discipline is developed through study and practice of Vinaya; concentration is developed through study and practice of Abhidharma; and wisdom is developed through study and practice of the Sūtras.
*Togpa,* realization, is brought forth from *lung*, the transmitted teachings, through the cultivation of three forms of wisdom. The steps in a traditional path of learning begin with the devoted study of these priceless teachings of the lineages of transmission, with careful reading and faithful listening. Next, what has been studied must be comprehensively examined in deliberate, concentrated reflection. Then what has been examined must be internalized in meditation, in an internal alchemical process that can be likened to laboratory testing of the insights gained through study and contemplation. These three steps are known as *thupai sherab (thos pa’i shes rab),* the wisdom born of study; *sampai sherab (bsam pa’i shes rab),* the wisdom born of investigation and contemplation; and *gompai sherab (sgom pa’i shes rab),* the wisdom born of deep meditation. Together, they constitute an invaluable approach that shows us how to directly apply to our experience what is presented in the Buddhavacana and the enlightened treatises. Thus, through cultivation of the Three Wisdoms, the teachings transmitted through the Buddha’s words are transformed into realization, with no hesitation, confusion, or doubts remaining.

Thanks to centuries of effort and extraordinary dedication on the part of Tibetan practitioners, scholars, and teachers, the twofold Dharma of *lung* and *togpa* has been held in trust for the sentient beings of our time: More than 1,100 texts of the Buddhadharma have been preserved in Tibetan translation in the great collection known as the Kangyur. Compiled as a collection in the fourteenth century, these texts relate in detail how the Buddha saw with perfect clarity into the heart of our experience, revealing the suffering and confusion that cause the wheel of existence to turn without cease. The Buddha’s teachings are said to embrace 84,000 discrete topics; a glimpse of their scope can be seen in the nine sections of the Derge Edition of the Kangyur. The editor of the Derge Kangyur, Situ Paṇchen, distinguished these teachings into two main groupings of exoteric scripture and esoteric tantra; the first five sections belong to the former grouping, while the last four belong to the latter grouping.
The eight texts of the first section, Discipline (*Vinaya;* *’dul ba*), present the Buddha’s advice regarding conduct for the Sangha, First Turning rules and guidelines that elucidate the subtleties of cause and effect. They include the Pratimokṣa vows for monks, nuns, laymen and laywomen, novices, and temporary vow-holders. The 37 texts of the Perfection of Wisdom section (*Prajñāpāramitā; sher phyin*) include all the Perfection of Wisdom texts of the Second Turning, including the well-known 100,000-line, 25,000-line, 18,000-line, 10,000-line, and 8,000-line versions. The Flower Ornament Sūtra (*Avataṃsaka; phal chen*) comprises the third section of the Derge Kangyur. One of the most important of the Third Turning Sūtras, this vast text informed the development of the Yogācāra, and includes the Praṇidhānarāja or Aspiration Prayer of Samantabhadra, first translated into Tibetan by Nanam Yeshe De. The 49 texts of the fourth section, the Heap of Jewels (*Ratnakūṭa; dkon brtsegs*) include some of the most important Sūtras of the Great Vehicle, the Mahāyāna. Together, they offer a comprehensive overview of philosophy, conduct, and practice according to the teachings of the Third Turning. The fifth section, Discourses (*Sūtra; mdo sde*), collects 274 Sūtras or discourses of the Buddha, precious scriptures of all traditions.
The sixth section, the Tantras (*Tantra; rgyud*), begins the portion of the Kangyur that presents the teachings of the Secret Mantra. This collection of 475 texts gathers the precious esoteric teachings brought to Tibet during the new translation period of the eleventh to thirteenth centuries–the Action and Conduct Tantras, the Yoga Tantras, and the Unsurpassed Yoga Tantras, distinguished as Mother, Father, and Nondual according to their emphasis on wisdom (prajñā; shes rab), method (upāya; thabs), or equal attention to both. The seventh section, entitled Ancient Tantras (*Pratantra; rnying rgyud*), gathers 25 texts, a small portion of the tantras that were transmitted to and translated by the Ngagyur Nyingma in the eighth and ninth centuries. The Wheel of Time section (*Kālacakra; ’dus kyi ’khor lo*) concerns the *Kālacakra Tantra,* brought to Tibet in the new translation era and transmitted by Sakya Paṇḍita, Butön, Tāranātha, and many others; it contains the *Vimalaprabhā* of Pundarika, King of Shambhala, the most important Kālacakra commentary studied in Tibet. The final section, Dharani (*Dhāraṇī; gzungs*), contains 270 mantras and formulas whose syllables have powerful transformative properties; their recitation heals and protects sentient beings.
In his words, the Buddha shows us how we may become free from suffering, step by step, in five paths known as the *Pañcāmārga (lam lnga)*: graduated paths of Preparation, Joining, Insight, Meditation, and No More Learning. The noble sons of the Tathāgata, the great bodhisattvas, practiced in this way, as did the Eighty-four Siddhas. Guru Padmasambhava, who it is said took birth eight years after the Jīna’s Parinirvāṇa, revealed the conduct of a second Buddha, demonstrating the path in clear signs for the wise. In Tibet, supported by Chögyal Trisong Detsen and guided by Guru Padmasambhava and Abbot Śāntarakṣita, the first sublime translators of the texts of the Kangyur followed in their footsteps. For more than 1,200 years, the precious teachings were held secure in the stronghold of the Snow Land. Eight great lineages of realization brought from India flourished there, held by masters of the Nyingma, the Kadampa, the Kagyu, the Sakya, the Gelug, the Jonang, and countless branches and practice lineages. Each has its own distinctive and valuable approach to study and practice, its own way to present and examine the teachings. The masters who founded the Tibetan schools studied and practiced with wisdom, compassion, and a depth of experience that could match that of the Bodhisattvas of the Indian heartland. Thanks to them, the precious heritage of Buddhavacana is available to us today.

*The Tibetan Inheritance*

Tibet’s history is long, and its links with Buddhist India may be ancient indeed. The first Emperor of Tibet, Nyatri Tsenpo, who reigned in the Yarlung Valley some 2,150 years ago, is said in certain early accounts to have been a scion of the Buddha’s own Śākya clan. In the fifth century, as the Dharma flourished throughout Asia, Lha-tho-tho-ri, the 28<sup>th</sup> Emperor of Tibet, was the first of the Yarlung Kings to encounter the Buddhavacana when he received texts and symbols of the Dharma. Arriving in an era before Tibet had a written script, this compact grouping of scriptures and sacred objects is said to have included a copy of the *Karaṇḍavyūha Sūtra,* containing Avalokiteśvara’s Maṇi Mantra, and the Pangkong Chak Gyepa, or “the 100 Salutations Repairing Breaches.” The Yarlung King carefully preserved these teachings, giving them the name Nyenpo Sangwa, the ‘Powerful Mystery.’ They were held in trust for some two hundred years, a treasure of the Land of Snow, until the seventh century, when, according to Butön, Thonmi Sambhoṭa, minister to the Avalokiteśvara-emanation Dharma king Songtsen Gampo, rendered them into Tibetan using the script that he designed at the request of his king, becoming the first *lotsāwa (lo tsā ba),* or translator, of written Tibetan in our history. This son of the Thonmi clan was reckoned a genius by the paṇḍitas with whom he studied for some seven years. The writing system he developed was likely based on Brahmi and Gupta scripts in use in Kashmir, but contained thirty syllables rather than the traditional thirty-four; he also designated six letters for sounds not represented in Sanskrit scripts. Along with the first Tibetan translations of scriptures, he also produced the first Tibetan grammatical works. Just as the Buddhist queens of Songtsen Gampo, Bhṛkuti Devi Tritsun of Nepal and Wencheng Kongjo of China, helped to lay the foundations for the adoption of the Dharma by sponsoring the construction of the land-taming temples of Tibet, Thonmi Sambhoṭa’s great creation serves as the basis for all scriptural study and practice in the Land of Snow.
A little more than a hundred years after the passing of Songtsen Gampo in 650, his lineal descendant, the 38<sup>th</sup> Emperor of Tibet, embarked upon a translation project that has scarcely been matched in history. The Mañjuśrī-manifestation Trisong Detsen, the Second Dharma King, governed a land vast in scope and riches. The Tibetan Empire, initially expanded across the Tibetan Plateau by the conquests of Songtsen Gampo, encompassed by the time of Trisong Detsen one of the largest territories in Asia, comparable in size to Tang China. Tibet controlled a crucial portion of the Silk Road, including the important trading city of Dunhuang; its power extended west as far as Gilgit, south into portions of India, Sikkim, Nepal, and Bhutan, north to Khotan and Kucha, and east nearly to Chang’an, the capital city of the Tang.
Trisong Detsen’s father, Tride Tsugtsen, known as Me Agtsom, the 37<sup>th</sup> Emperor of Tibet (704–755), had been a supporter of Buddhism, as was his Chinese Queen, the devout Kimsheng Kongjo, who helped fund temples and generously offered shelter to Khotanese monks. When an epidemic struck Tibet, taking the life of the queen, the king’s ministers blamed the royal patronage of the Dharma; as a result, Me Agtsom too lost his life. Succeeding his father at the age of 13, Trisong Detsen ruled quietly until he reached the age of 20, when he revealed his intention to establish Buddhism as the official religion of Tibet.
Ruling at the apex of his kingdom’s temporal power, Trisong Detsen transformed the spiritual destiny of the Snow Land. He invited to his kingdom the Bodhisattva-Abbot Śāntarakṣita, perhaps the greatest living philosopher of the Indian Buddhist tradition, and the Great Guru Padmasambhava, the supremely powerful Mantrayāna-master from the northwest land of Oḍḍiyāna. Born as brothers in the time of the previous Buddha Kaśyapa, the Guru, the Abbot and the King fulfilled their vow to secure Tibet in the Dharma: the Lopön Padmasambhava tamed the spirits of the land, enabling the Chögyal to build the monastery of Samye; and the Khenpo Śāntarakṣita bestowed the Rāhula-Vinaya of the Mūlasarvāstivāda school upon Tibetans, beginning with the first seven in that land ever to hold the monastic ordination, including the minister Ba Selnang and the master translator Vairotsana. As the Tibetan lotsāwas mastered Sanskrit, the Bodhisattva Abbot propounded the Sūtra teachings of cause and effect, transmitting the scriptures and treatises of Vinaya, Prajñāpāramitā, and Tathāgatagarbha. He instructed lotsāwas in the complexities of the Indian philosophical traditions, grounding them firmly in the Abhidharma works of Asaṅga and Vasubandhu, treatises that assessed the Vaibhāṣika and the Sautrāntika, the Cittamātra-Yogācāra of Maitreya and Asaṅga, and the writings of the Madhyamikas, including the works of Nāgārjuna and Āryadeva, as well as teachings distinguished by the translator Nanam Zhang Yeshe De into two main streams: the Sautrāntika-Svātantrika Madhyamika, as found in the writings of Bhāviveka, and the Yogācāra-Svātantrika-Madhyamika of Śāntarakṣita.
At the same time, Guru Padmasambhava taught the resultant vehicle to a small number of direct disciples, transmitting the Mantrayāna teachings of Oḍḍiyāna and India, which had the power to bring realization in a single lifetime. These included the Inner Yogas of Mahā, Anu and Atiyoga, and Chiti and Yangti, steps beyond Atiyoga that are not commonly known. Guru Padma initiated the king in the sādhanas of the Eight Herukas, and gave countless teachings of the Mahā and Anuyoga to twenty-five chief disciples. In this way, he first brought to Tibet the teachings of the Magical Net, encompassing the great *Guhyagarbha Tantra,* known in Tibet as the Sangwai Nyingpo, as well as precious Unsurpassed Yoga Tantras such as the *Mañjuśrī-nāma-saṃgīti.* It seems that Padmasambhava’s personal annotated copy of this precious text was kept at Samye, where Atiśa Dīpaṃkāra Śrījñāna is said to have personally witnessed it when he visited at the dawn of the new translation era. Having received all the Atiyoga Heart Essence teachings transmitted to Garab Dorje, the first human to hold the full realization of a Vidyādhara, and continued by Mañjuśrīmitra, Śrī Siṃha, Jñānasūtra, and Vimalamitra, Padmasambhava transmitted them to close disciples including Khandro Yeshe Tsogyal, and to the daughter of Trisong Detsen, Princess Pema Sel. These Khandro Nyingthig teachings, hidden as a treasure, would later be revealed by the emanation Pema Ladrel Sel, the prior incarnation of Omniscient Longchenpa.
In this way, under the extraordinarily beneficial conditions engendered by the activities of the three great masters known as Khen Lop Chö Sum, both the Sūtrayāna and Mantrayāna were established in Tibet.

Great Indian paṇḍitas assisted in this endeavor. Vimalamitra journeyed to Tibet and trained numerous Tibetan disciples, including the translators Kawa Paltsek and Ma Rinchen Chok; his Bima Nyingthig Atiyoga lineage would form a central part of the heritage of the Ngagyur Nyingma. The Mahāsiddha Līlāvajra guided the lotsāwas, and wrote extensive commentaries on the *Guhyagarbha Tantra* and the *Mañjuśrī-nāma-saṃgīti.* His disciple Buddhaguhya held the lineages of Yoga tantras he received in Oḍḍiyāna, including the texts of the Magical Net and the *Dupai Do,* the Sūtra Gathering the Intentions of All the Buddhas. On retreat at Mount Kailash, he received a delegation from Trisong Detsen, who sent several of his ministers and three large measures of gold to invite him to Tibet; while he chose to remain at Kailash, he gave extensive initiations to the envoys, and sent his own commentarial writings on several tantras, including an important *lamrim,* or exposition of the path, for the *Guhyagarbha Tantra.* He is also traditionally regarded as the composer of an extensive letter of guidance for the Dharma King, his ministers, practitioners, and monks. Another master who assisted in the transmission was Huṃkāra, ordained by Buddhajñāna at Nālandā, one of the Eight Vidyādharas of India and holder of the Yangdak Heruka sādhanas; he is said to have become the teacher of Trisong Detsen’s youngest son, King Senalek.
The flowering of Dharma in Tibet was timely, for only a few centuries after the founding of Samye, in the tumult that struck the Indian subcontinent at the end of the 1100s, the great mahāvihāras that held the concentrated wealth of centuries of Buddhist knowledge were sacked by invaders from the northwest. According to Tāranātha, whose history of Indian Buddhism appeared in 1608, only seventy monks remained at Nālandā after this great destruction ran its course; within just a handful of years, in the places where tens of thousands once studied and practiced, only ruins remained. It is said that the great libraries of Nālandā continued burning for months due to the quantity of texts they contained; due to the razing of the mahāvihāras, virtually the entire Indian Buddhist text tradition—a heritage of Sanskrit texts precious beyond price—was lost.
Many of these masterworks survive only in Tibetan translation. Thanks to the hundreds of lotsāwas who worked with great dedication in both the earlier and later periods, the Tibetan scriptural tradition offers authentic and reliable access to these ancient teachings. Among the great Buddhist canons that survive today, the Tibetan canon is widely considered to provide a uniquely comprehensive view of millennia of Indian teachings, capturing the full scope of the Dharma from its earliest expressions through its full flowering. From the start, Tibetans have devoted exceptional care and energy to the act of translation, carrying out their work with deep respect for the original texts as well as unmatched mastery of the teachings they relate. In the eighth century, the era of Khen Lop Chö Sum, more than a hundred Indian paṇḍitas and Tibetan lotsāwas worked together on these texts, most of which would centuries later be compiled in the Kangyur, while some were gathered into the Tengyur, the translated works of the great Indian commentators. With policies designed to maintain the highest standards in the translations, the successors of Trisong Detsen–Mune Tsenpo, Senalek and the Third Dharma King, the Vajrapāṇi-emanation Tri Ralpachen–firmly protected the integrity of the teachings.
Special care was taken with the esoteric teachings brought into Tibet in this period. They were not made public or widely disseminated, for strict rules governing their translation were set in place by the Dharma Kings to help guarantee their continued purity and efficacy. Following modes of study, practice, and conduct that had protected the teachings for centuries in India, the direct students of Guru Padmasambhava, Paṇchen Vimalamitra, and Lochen Vairotsana became inheritors of a vast and profound tradition. Accounts and chronicles detailing these transmission lineages have in more recent times been supplemented by very early texts found in the library cave at Dunhuang, whose treasures were revealed in the early twentieth century.
Trisong Detsen’s assistant, the monk Nyang Tingdzin Zangpo, held the lineage for the Bima Nyingthig of Vimalamitra, a lineage of heart-essence wisdom that was carried to Dangma Lhungyel and then to Chetsun Sengge Wangchuk. From this master the transmission passed in turn to Zhangton, Nyima Bum, Guru Jober, Menlungpa Mikyo Dorje, Melong Dorje, Trulshik Sengge Gyepa, and Kumaradza, who bestowed it upon his heart-son, the Omniscient Longchenpa. The sky-like mind of Longchenpa held the union of the teachings of Guru Padma’s Khandro Nyingthig and Vimalamitra’s Bima Nyingthig; their transmission leads to transformation, the enlightened embodiment that is sometimes called the rainbow body. From Padmasambhava’s disciple Nyak Jñānakumara, the Kama or scriptural basis of the Ngagyur Nyingma passed to Nubchen Sangye Yeshe, who traveled west as far as Gilgit for the sake of the teachings. From Nubchen they passed to So Yeshe Wangchuk, and from him to the great early masters of the Zur line, Zurchen Śākya Jungne, Zurchungpa, and Zur Śākya Sengge, known as Dropukpa; over the centuries, they preserved this vital scriptural tradition at their retreat-place of Ukpalung.
At about the time of Zurpoche, at the dawn of the new translation era, the great Nyingma scholar and translator Rongzom Mahāpaṇḍita held the lineages of Padmasambhava and Vairotsana, and received as well the lineage of the ninth-century master Aro Yeshe Jungne, wisdom-inheritor of seven successive masters of India and seven successive masters of China. Rongzompa became one of the first Tibetans to compose extensive learned treatises in Sanskrit, in the manner of the paṇḍitas of India, who accorded his works great respect. Through the Zurs, the Rong tradition of the Great Perfection (Atiyoga, or Dzogchen) took root in Central Tibet, to be transmitted intact across the centuries until it reached Zur Chöying Rangdrol, who gave it to the Great Fifth Dalai Lama in the seventeenth century. In the twelfth century, an eastern stream of the Great Perfection teachings emerged when Dropukpa’s disciple transmitted the Mahā, Anu, and Atiyogas to the brilliant Dampa Deshek, who founded Katok Dorje Den, stronghold of the Kham Dzogchen Lineage and eldest of the Nyingma Mother Monasteries, in 1159. More than 100,000 rainbow-body masters formed part of the Katok tradition, which over the centuries would nourish the development of the later Nyingma Mother Monasteries of Kham. The Rong and Kham traditions were both in time transmitted to the great Vairotsana-emanation Orgyen Terdag Lingpa, founder of the Mother Monastery of Mindrolling; together with Dorje Drak and Palri Thekchen Ling, this revered center for study and practice upheld the scriptural Kama tradition and the teachings known as the precious Terma in Central Tibet.
The Terma, or treasure teachings, of Guru Padmasambhava were hidden as specific manifestations of the Lotus Guru’s blessing that would emerge at predicted places and times to the holders of his lineage. With compassionate and far-seeing eyes, Guru Padma perceived the need for the esoteric traditions to be continually renewed as centuries of history and shifting circumstance took their toll; with the aid of Khandro Yeshe Tsogyal, he ensured the continued presence of his blessings in Tibet. Buried in every part of the Snow Land, they were revealed by countless Tertöns, radiant emanations of his direct disciples, including the Three Supreme Emanations of Chögyal Trisong Detsen: Nyangrel Nyima Odzer, Terchen Guru Chökyi Wangchuk, and Rigdzin Godem. Treasures of teachings in sādhana form and countless sacred biographies of Padmasambhava were discovered by the 108 Great and 1,000 Minor Tertöns, starting with Sangye Lama and including the masters known as the Eight Great Lingpas. In this way, two potent streams of teachings flowed forth from the time of the Dharma Kings. Held by great masters including Orgyen Terdag Lingpa, Jigme Lingpa, Jamyang Khyentse Wangpo, Jamgon Kongtrul, Chokyur Lingpa, Dudjom Lingpa, Kyabje Dudjom Rinpoche, Kyabje Dilgo Khyentse, and many others, both the Kama and Terma lineages have continued through to our present era.
While the esoteric traditions of the time of Khen Lop Chö Sum are generally upheld within the Nyingma tradition, the Sūtrayāna texts translated during this period became the foundation for every one of the Tibetan Buddhist schools; for this accomplishment, the translators of the Ngagyur Nyingma are still well-known today. Nanam Yeshe De translated hundreds of texts, including more than 160 Sūtras. The scope of this master-lotsāwa is demonstrated by the broad range of works he translated, including Sūtras, Śāstras, Abhidharma and Prajñāpāramitā, the texts of the Mādhyamikas, and numerous Nyingma Tantras. Kawa Paltsek Rakṣita, ordained by Śāntarakṣita, was by some accounts among those sent by Trisong Detsen to invite Vimalamitra to Tibet. He translated or revised around 130 texts, including the Āgamas, Mahāyāna Sūtras and Śāstras, Asaṅga’s Abhidharma, and Prajñāpāramitā texts, as well as the first Tibetan translation of the *Bodhicaryavatāra*. Chokro Lui Gyaltsen, likewise a disciple of Padmasambhava and Vimalamitra, translated some fifty works that included sixteen Prajñāpāramitā texts, the *Abhidharma-samuccaya* of Asaṅga, the *Abhidharma-koṣa* of Vasubandhu, and works of the Mādhyamika masters, including the encyclopedic *Tattvasaṃgraha* of Abbot Śāntarakṣita, which would influence Tibetan thought for centuries. Nyak Jñānakumara, great upholder of the Kama lineage, was ordained by Śāntarakṣita and became a close disciple of Padmasambhava and Vimalamitra. He helped to translate some of the most important esoteric teachings of the Nyingma tradition. Ma Rinchen Chok, who like Nyak and Kawa Peltsek was one of the first Tibetans to receive full ordination, translated many esoteric texts and authored hundreds of commentaries on the works of his teachers Padmasambhava, Vimalamitra, and Buddhaguhya, although only a few have survived to the present day. He is said to have aided Guru Padma and Yeshe Tsogyal in their efforts to conceal the Terma teachings throughout the land of Tibet.
The greatest of these translators was Lochen Pagor Vairotsana, one of the chief disciples of Guru Padmasambhava. Said to have been discovered by the Lotus Guru at the age of eight and sent to the Āryadeśa in search of esoteric teachings at the age of fifteen, Vairotsana was instrumental in the transmission of the Inner Yogas to Tibet. His biographers relate that he traveled widely in India, studying with many great masters including the Vidyādhara Sri Siṃha, before returning to Tibet with hundreds of sacred texts. His name is found in the colophons of twenty-one works in the Kangyur, including the 100,000-line Prajñāpāramitā, yet this picture of his activities appears to be incomplete; even today, a definitive catalog of all the works translated during the time of Khen Lop Chö Sum remains challenging to establish. When, at the end of the tenth century, Tibet reforged its links with the living masters of Buddhist India, a new wave of translations began, spearheaded by Lochen Rinchen Zangpo, who carried out more than 150 translations of Sūtras, Śāstras, and Tantras, including the precious *Mañjuśrī-nāma-saṃgīti.* Yet even as new generations of translators worked with erudition and great diligence on the texts of practice lineages emerging from India, the significance of the accomplishments of the Imperial Age was well understood. So much can be discerned in the poem of praise composed by the great Ngok Lotsāwa Loden Sherab. Born 965 years ago in the early Sarma period, he honored the translators who came before him, including the masters of the Ngagyur Nyingma:

Be ro tsa na nam mkha’i mtha’ dang mnyam
Ska cho zhang gsum nyi zla zung cig ’brel

Rin chen bzang po tho rengs skar chen tsam
Kho bo cag ni srin bu me khyer bzhin
Vairotsana was equal to the reaches of space,
Ka[wa] and Chok[ro] like sun and moon, Zhang [Yeshe De] their union,
Rinchen Zangpo the great morning star—
Compared to them, we are mere fireflies.

Ngok Lotsāwa’s verse invites us to look again at the foundations laid for Tibetan Dharma in the time of the Guru, the Abbot, and the King. Without Śāntarakṣita, the Mūlasarvāstivādin Vinaya upheld throughout Tibet would not have taken root. Without Guru Padmasambhava and Paṇchen Vimalamitra, the esoteric traditions that flourished when Indian Dharma was at its greatest height would not have found a home in the Snow Land, establishing Tibet in the Vajrayāna. Without the Dharma King’s determination to translate the Buddhavacana into Tibetan, the treasures of Sūtra and Śāstra would not have been gathered at Samye, where Atiśa Dipaṃkāra would later find Sanskrit texts of ancient teachings that had not been preserved even at Nālandā. The preservation efforts of scholars of the early Sarma, starting in the tenth century, were often supported by the work of the early translators through whom flowed the first wave of teachings. This can be seen clearly, for example, in the body of work of the great Chomden Rigpai Reldri of Narthang Monastery, who among his countless accomplishments to benefit the Doctrine authenticated the Sanskrit original of the *Guhyagarbha Tantra*.
*Journey of the Kangyur*
As Sarma translators continued their activity and new practice lineages were established, a prodigious number of teachings were transmitted to Tibet. By the early 1270s, Chomden Rigrel had completed a survey of the textual inheritance brought to Tibet from India and elsewhere, listing more than 2,000 texts that would eventually fill the Kangyur and its associated commentaries, the Tengyur, including Sūtras, dhāraṇīs, Vinaya and Abhidharma texts, śāstras, stotras, and tantras. Among the many sources Chomden Rigrel consulted were directories known as karchags, including two famous examples compiled in the time of the Dharma Kings: the Denkarma, containing some 737 titles, and the Phangthangma, containing around 960 titles. Around 1310, his disciples compiled the collections known as the Old Narthang Kangyur and Tengyur, regarded by many as the archetype of the Tibetan Buddhist Canon as it appears today. Working at Zhalu Monastery some decades after Chomden Rigrel, the celebrated scholar and translator Butön Rinchen Drub edited and expanded the Old Narthang collections.
Inspired by these accomplishments, great scholars made heroic efforts to edit and refine comprehensive collections of the Buddhavacana. These Kangyur collections are often characterized in two main lineages of descent: the eastern ‘Tshalpa’ line, named for a manuscript produced at Tshal Gungthang and consecrated by Butön in 1351; and the western ‘Thempangma’ line, stemming from a well-edited manuscript of 1431 produced in Gyantse and sponsored by Gyantse’s ruler, Rabten Kunzang Pakpa. Independent Kangyurs are also known, whose origins cannot be traced directly to either line. These include very early partial collections found at Tabo and Gondhla; the volumes of the Bathang Kangyur found in Kham, dating from the fifteenth to sixteenth centuries; the 1699–1700 Tawang Kangyur, containing some sixty Nyingma tantras, found at Orgyen Ling in the family temple of the Sixth Dalai Lama; and the 119-volume Phugdrak Kangyur, completed in 1706 in Ngari.
The first xylographic or block print Kangyur, the 105-volume Yong Le, emerged from the eastern Tshalpa line. Sponsored by the Yong Le Emperor, the third emperor of the Ming dynasty, it was carved in Nanjing, China in 1410. A distinct lineage of block print Kangyurs descended from this edition, including the Wanli of 1605, in 105 volumes with 42 supplementary volumes, sponsored by the Wanli Emperor Shenzong, the thirteenth emperor of the Ming dynasty, and printed from the blocks of the Yong Le; the 105-volume Kangxi, produced between 1684 and 1692, with new blocks that duplicated the blocks of the Yong Le; and the 107-volume Peking-Qianlong Kangyur of 1737, produced during the reign of the Qianlong Emperor. Other block print Kangyurs trace their origins to the Chingwa Taktse manuscript that is believed to have been based on the Tshalpa manuscript Kangyur; these include the 109-volume Lithang or Jang Satham Kangyur of 1609–1614, edited by the Sixth Shamarpa, Mipham Chökyi Wangchuk. The first block print Kangyur produced within Tibet, its carving was carried out in Jang, in present-day Lithang County, Kham, and sponsored by its king. The Lithang Kangyur served as the basis for the 108-volume Choné Kangyur of 1731, whose carving was sponsored by the Choné leader Makzor Gonpo and carried out in the Choné region of Amdo.
The ‘western’ Kangyur lineage is reflected in a series of manuscript copies of the Thempangma, including a 114-volume copy offered to the Mongols in 1671, known today as the Ulan Bator; the 105-volume Shelkar Chöde Kangyur, a copy made in 1712 of a Thempangma manuscript copy held in Dingri; and the 109-volume Ladakh Tog Kangyur of 1729, based on a Bhutanese manuscript copy of the Thempangma, with the work carried out at the Tog Palace in Leh during the reign of Nyima Namgyel. In addition, a copy of the Thempangma produced in 1858 and held in Japan has come to be known as the Tōyō Bunko Kangyur.

Over time, these lineages contributed to the creation of Kangyurs with characteristics from both traditions. The 101-volume Narthang Kangyur of 1731 was initially sponsored and directed by Desi Sangye Gyatso, regent of the Great Fifth Dalai Lama, with the project finally completed with the support of Polhané Sonam Topgye, who guided Tibet in the period following the civil wars of the early eighteenth century; the carving was carried out at Narthang Monastery during the tenure of the Sixth Dalai Lama. The celebrated 103-volume Derge Edition was produced after careful study of examples that included the Lho rDzong Thempangma manuscript and the Jang block print of the Tshalpa lineage. Edited by the great Situ Paṇchen and completed in 1733, it was sponsored by Tenpa Tsering, the Tenth King of Derge; its carving was carried out at Derge Parkhang. Copies of this highly-regarded and influential edition became widespread in Tibet. It was reproduced in the 1820 Ragya Kangyur, and in the Mongolian Sog Khuré or Urga Kangyur, produced in Outer Mongolia at Khuré Monastery between 1908 and 1910. The final Kangyur to be produced in Old Tibet, the 100-volume Zhol or Lhasa Kangyur, was produced in Lhasa in 1934.
Following the invasion of Tibet, refugees strove to preserve their sacred texts in a time of unprecedented danger to the tradition. While a handful of sets of the Kangyur had been placed in Western university libraries, grave concerns remained about its future survival and dissemination. It is a testament to the tireless work of those who care for the Kangyur that new editions have been produced in the decades since the invasion, helping to renew its promise of perfect refuge for all sentient beings.
Over the years, numerous important preservation efforts have taken place in Asia. In the late 1970s, His Holiness the Sixteenth Karmapa sponsored a special reprint of a ‘par phud’ or ‘first fruits’ edition of the Derge Kangyur that had been a treasure of the Thirteenth Karmapa; this work was carried out in Delhi. Between 2006 and 2009, the 108-volume Pedurma Comparative Kangyur was produced. Printed in Chengdu, this Derge-lineage edition lists text variants from eight distinct block print recensions of the Kangyur. In 2016, continuing the work of his honored predecessor, His Holiness the Seventeenth Karmapa published a digital edition of the Lithang/Jang Kangyur.
At the same time, in the West, as a backstop against the Tibetan Buddhist Canon’s disappearance, my students and I created the Nyingma Edition of the Kangyur and Tengyur in 1981. This augmented facsimile copy of the Derge Edition block print, printed in Berkeley, CA, added to the 1,141 titles of the Derge Kangyur numerous texts not found in that edition, compiled in 36 atlas-sized Western volumes. Sets of this edition presently reside in monastic and university libraries around the world. A two-volume key was produced for ready reference to the Nyingma Edition, and an extended eight-volume bibliography was developed to support scholarly research.
When I founded the Nyingma Monlam Chenmo in 1989, I recognized an opportunity to support the Tibetan textual tradition by offering volumes of crucial sacred texts, from the Kangyur and other sources, to the participants who gathered in Bodh Gaya to chant for world peace. Having just completed its 36th year, the ceremony has become a key site for the free distribution of more than 20,000 individual sacred texts compiled, formatted, and printed by the Yeshe De Project. These efforts have allowed about five and a half million books to reach more than three thousand monasteries, nunneries, retreat places, and Dharma centers in India, Nepal, Bhutan, the lands of the Himalayas, and the border regions of Tibet. In 1994, to foster the continuity of our shared traditions and the study and practice of the Buddhavacana, I sponsored large prayer monlams for all the Tibetan schools; in 1994 and 1995, Kagyu ceremonies were held at Lumbini, Sakya ceremonies took place at Lumbini and Boudhanath, and Gelug ceremonies were conducted at Sarnath, with a Four Schools Monlam held at Boudhanath.
In recent years, recognizing the critical importance of the Kangyur and hoping to foster its continued study in coming generations, Yeshe De has distributed more than ten thousand sets of the Kangyur to the Ārya Sangha. This number includes several important historic editions, including a typeset Derge Kangyur, offered between 2002 and 2011; a printing of the Narthang Kangyur; and a facsimile printing of the Tog Palace Kangyur. In 2012, we issued the Yeshe De Yidzhin Norbu Kangyur, a new typeset augmented Derge Kangyur in 133 Western-style volumes; this edition has since been re-released in a traditional loose-leaf (poti) version comprising 108 volumes. The Yidzhin Norbu Kangyur contains texts not found in the Derge Edition, including the *Dhammapada* translated into Tibetan, and the *Mahāvibhasa-sūtra* in Chinese. To benefit scholars, researchers and students, we have included sixteen historic karchags, beginning with the Denkarma and Phangthangma, and the *Two-Volume Lexicon* (*Madhyavyutpatti*) compiled during the reigns of Senalek and Tri Ralpachen. In addition, we included karchags of Chomden Rigrel (1227–1305), Jetsun Drakpa Gyaltsen (1147–1216), Drogön Chögyal Pakpa (1235–1280), Butön Rinchen Drub (1290–1364), and Drikung Rigdzin Chödrak (1595–1662); the karchag produced by Situ Paṇchen (1699–1774) for the Derge Kangyur; and karchags of the Ulan Bator Thempangma, Yongle, Lithang, Peking, Narthang, Choné, Sog Khure and Zhol Kangyurs. The *reu mik,* a special set of tables unique to the Yidzhin Norbu Kangyur, draws upon sixteen distinct canonical collections, providing comparative information for each text from the block print Yong Le, Lithang, Peking, Choné, Narthang, Derge, Ragya, Sog Khure, and Zhol editions; the manuscript Ulan Bator, Shelkar, and Tog Palace Kangyurs; and the independent Phugdrak Kangyur. The *reu mik* also includes references for the 1981 Nyingma Edition, the 2009 Pedurma Comparative Edition, and the 2012 edition of the Yidzhin Norbu.
*The Kangyur Karchag Encyclopedia*
We are honored that the Yidzhin Norbu and its scholarly tools could play a helping role in the development of the Kangyur Karchag Encyclopedia.
This project has been decades in the planning. Some tentative steps towards a descriptive Kangyur karchag were taken in the U.S. in the 1970s, when three of my students who had some familiarity with Tibetan and Sanskrit began to catalog texts for the Nyingma Edition; they also prepared Chinese references for certain titles. This preliminary effort to summarize the Kangyur texts was cursory by necessity, consisting of only two or three lines per entry; but it planted a seed that I hoped would someday grow and blossom. When I created the Khen Lop Chö Sum Foundation in 2014 at the Sarnath International Nyingma Institute (SINI), with the goal of supporting scholarly research into the era of the Dharma Kings, it opened up an opportunity for highly trained khenpos and geshes to devote their full attention to unfolding the meanings of the Kangyur’s texts and colophons. Sarnath has long been held sacred as the place where the Buddha first turned the wheel of the Dharma; it is a most auspicious home for a project devoted to helping the wider world appreciate and understand the precious Buddhavacana.
Work on the Kangyur Karchag Encyclopedia began in 2018. Now it is coming to fruition. The text summaries it contains have been formulated to benefit students and scholars of a wide range of levels, providing a firm basis for exegesis as well as an accessible introduction for newcomers. With straightforward topics and clearly delineated sections, the Kangyur becomes easier to study, allowing teachers and students to draw out and distinguish the literal, general, hidden, and ultimate levels of meaning in a text. It has been carefully proofread numerous times. This is truly a wonderful result, and I am glad to see it receive support from His Holiness the Fourteenth Dalai Lama, His Holiness the Forty-First Sakya Trichen, His Holiness Drikung Kyabgon Chetsang Rinpoche, His Eminence Samdhong Rinpoche, and His Eminence Thrangu Tulku. Special thanks are due to His Eminence Dzongsar Khyentse Rinpoche, whose heartfelt support through the Khyentse Foundation has allowed the Encyclopedia to be brought to completion.

Rejoicing in their efforts, I wish to express my deep appreciation for the editorial team of the Kangyur Karchag Encyclopedia, an all-schools group of some fifty individuals guided by Khenpo Ju Tenkyong, Khenpo Thubten Lobsang, Khenpo Thupten Gyatso, and Khenpo Tsering Nyima. I am delighted that this group also includes lamas from my home monastery, Tarthang. For the last several years, these learned and sincere khenpos, geshes, lamas, and teachers have worked very hard, studying the scriptures, carefully researching countless topics, and editing the Encyclopedia, with the help of my youngest daughter, Tsering Palmo Gellek, a skillful communicator whose wise, foresighted and steady management of this important project has enabled them to create a new bridge to the treasures of the Kangyur. Masters of all the schools can now build on the foundation that has been created here, helping to ensure the continuity of the distinctive interpretive approaches that have guided study and practice for centuries. Going forward, I hope that learned scholars from every school will suggest corrections and improvements to the Encyclopedia, contributing additional analyses that will shed more light on the texts of the Kangyur for future readers. As the project continues to grow, I pray that this Encyclopedia will serve not only to nurture the ancient commentarial traditions, but also to form a support for the translation of the Buddhavacana into many new languages. In undertaking such a project, scholars of all schools can help future students working in new Dharma languages to learn how to discern the subtle levels of meaning in these texts, so that the highest significance of the Kangyur may be revealed. Ultimately, it is my wish to see this effort expanded to encompass the more than three thousand texts of the Tengyur; for if the Kangyur is to be fully understood, a deep engagement with the Śāstras is also necessary.

*Taking the Teachings to Heart*

Through sincere, sustained study and practice of the scriptural tradition, undertaking the Three Trainings and cultivating the Three Wisdoms, we may come to discover that Buddha, Dharma, and Sangha are our own mind, breath, and body. Together, they express the path our energy takes, a journey from *lung* to *togpa,* from transmission to realization.

Taking this journey, we may come to understand that everything that appears to arise is *shimmering;* it all exists in a stage of transition. There is a profound difference between a week-old embryo and a fully-grown adult, and similar alterations mark the course of our entire human history on this planet. The characteristics and qualities of everything that manifests are temporary and contingent, not only what appears *to* us but our own appearance on the scene as well: those disparate elements we call sensing, feelings, perceptions, conceptual understanding, and consciousness. Subject and object seem well-established and fundamentally distinct from each other, yet upon closer examination, we find they are mutually dependent, deriving their seeming stability from one another in a constant interplay.
This interdependence has important implications for experience. Because these inner causal connections are not well understood, the insights gained from the study of appearances can become repatriated as concepts, with little potential to effect change. We make a statement of impermanence, saying, “We can’t live forever,” but our words float on top of a mystery; for we do not understand the transitioning of cause and effect, how and in what magical way appearances manifest, and to whom. Only when these deeper meanings are accessed can teachings like Prajñāpāramitā be truly understood. If we fail to discern these secret causal links and their relationship to śūnyatā, ‘emptiness’ becomes merely a label, taking its place in a network of labels that has captured the meanings of words like ‘joy’, ‘happiness’, and ‘freedom’ and rendered them powerless. When our understanding is founded on these descriptive tags, we may be left wondering how to manifest real insight.
When ‘I’ and ‘me’ preside over experience, that ‘how’ comes to dictate our understanding. Just as a king is attended by a retinue of ministers and courtiers, an assembly of observations surrounds the subject, showing ‘how’, pointing out this way and that, and feeding back according to custom. The I-sovereign, presented with evidence, with tributes, responds ‘yes’ and ‘no,’ all thought circumscribed in a system of discriminations based upon ‘I am’ and ‘my knowing.’ In this system, all knowing is known according to *me,* according to concept and conclusion, according to the subject of the analytical ‘how.’ That ‘how’ is how we study, and by means of ‘how,’ we may feel we have graduated: now we have a degree of knowledge. Firmly belonging to a school, we become its exponents, and pronounce ourselves masters. And yet, since we remain dependent on the mind and the manifestations of the material world–on the subject of identity and the object of appearance–the relative universe has the power to catch us in a bind. Our entrapment may make itself felt as obstacles to practice, fixed reflections of dreamlike residues that cannot be resolved unless and until we go very thoroughly into the three forms of wisdom, *thupai, sampai,* and *gompai sherab:* proceeding step by step into real understanding.
We may begin simply by considering the profundity of the Four Noble Truths. As we weigh the nature of human suffering and assess its causes, we can see that the poisons of craving, aversion, and ignorance have profound karmic impacts on sentient beings, leaving their marks upon experience. Guided by the insights of the Jīna, so well expressed in the scriptures, we can look deeply into ourselves, tracing the residues, known and unknown, left behind by karma and kleśa in our bodies and minds, in our spirits and our speech. Given power by *not-knowing*, these residues can become carriers that perpetuate samsara. Without ever realizing the way it sustains the cycle, the mind becomes mired in delusion, like an elephant trapped in mud. Just as its very strength and mass make it harder for the elephant to escape, the mind’s formidable power of manifestation lends its force to the patterns that entangle sentient beings.
Yet the perfect transformation of these seemingly intractable conditions is possible on the path taught by the Buddha: sentient beings can become entirely free from karma and kleśa. The union of wisdom and compassion embodied by the Tathāgata can transform even the most fundamental forms of confusion, penetrating the play of subject-mind and object-appearance that gives rise to what we call reality. Illuminating the not-knowing that permeates and permits this play, wisdom uncovers a field of pure openness in the Dharmakāya, whose joyful expression shines in the purified field of Sambhogakāya and is revealed in clear signs in the body, speech and mind of the precious Nirmāṇakāyas.
The essence of this understanding, the heart of the Buddhadharma, is found in the Kangyur; it delineates the unfailing path demonstrated by the sons of the Conqueror. Bodhisattvas like Mañjuśrī, Avalokiteśvara and Vajrapāṇi, and heroes like the valiant masters of our great traditions–these are our cherished models and guides, our Kalyāṇamitras, the good friends who show us the way. Many generations of great paṇḍitas and lotsāwas made efforts on our behalf to preserve and transmit this precious knowledge. As we continue this journey, as we help new students to cross the bridge to the Kangyur for the first time, let us continue to depend on these precious teachers.
It may ultimately be devotion that enables us to truly understand what the Buddha taught, for real study and practice call for a serious dedication to the process of transformation. While I myself am no scholar, in supporting and preserving the Kangyur, I have tried to do my best to protect the knowledge that enables human beings to arrive at true happiness. I hope we will continue to apply ourselves to the scriptures with real depth of feeling, giving our hearts, minds and spirits to the Buddha’s words, and not only our intellectual attention, so that we may experience the deep serenity that long study and practice can bring. In doing so, we give our fellow human beings an incomparable gift; knowing peace ourselves, we become able to share it with others.
Sharing this treasure is particularly important now. Those dedicated to study and practice in Asia face powerful cultural and historical currents that seem ready to sweep away their religious traditions, as a fascination with material gain becomes more and more widespread. In Tibet, restrictions on all forms of religious expression are tightening, and Tibetan children are no longer being educated in their native language. In this uncertain time, the Dharma’s next chapters may indeed be written in new lands, in new languages. In many places, a greater interest in Buddhism is developing, even as the situation in Tibet has declined.
On virtually every continent, as the Nyingma, Kagyu, Geluk, Sakya, Jonang and other Tibetan Buddhist schools have opened centers and branches, it seems that people are becoming more open to practices like meditation, yoga, and visualization. Initiatives like the 84,000 Project are an encouraging sign that a new world may be ready to embrace the doctrine. I hope that knowledgeable people from every country, every discipline, will take this chance to explore together the potential of a universal enlightenment. In particular, I urge readers from Western countries to examine, embrace and protect these teachings, so that they may continue to be available to coming generations, for the Dharma’s future may ultimately depend on its reception in the West. A sign of this is the success of our projects, for our work has been supported by precious American freedoms and open-minded, open-hearted Western students, friends, and allies, without whom projects like SINI, the Khen Lop Chö Sum Foundation, and the Kangyur Karchag Encyclopedia would not have been possible.
I write these words in my ninetieth year, having witnessed the utter transformation of Tibet and deeply concerned about the future of the Buddhadharma on our planet. I became a refugee when I left Tibet in 1958, following in the footsteps of my root guru, Kyabje Khyentse Dorje Chang. For more than sixty years I have tried in my limited way to help preserve Tibet’s inheritance of knowledge in Asia, while doing everything I can to support the introduction of the teachings to the lands of the West. To all the students, scholars, practitioners and translators who care for this heritage, I offer my warmest wishes. For my part, I earnestly dedicate any merit that may have been generated by my preservation efforts to my honored parents and dear family, my precious teachers, and all the sentient beings of the Six Realms and the Three Times. If you are new to the Kangyur, I invite you with all my heart to read the Buddhavacana. I sincerely believe that your reading the Buddha’s words will be worth your while, and could bring great benefit to the entire world.

It is up to us to convey to the future the twofold Dharma of *lung* and *togpa*. Because of their power to transform our suffering, it is critical that the Buddha’s words be read with care and energy, not only by those who traditionally study and practice Tibetan Dharma, but by ever-widening circles of scholars, professionals, and laypeople from all parts of the world. How valuable might these treasures be to our time? How many of the world’s people are suffering today, even the most affluent, educated, and privileged among us? For our samsaric suffering, there is no question that the finest antidote available is the Buddhavacana. The healing potency of these teachings goes right to the heart of our sorrow, showing us the way to transcend the ignorance that restricts our understanding, so that skandhas, āyatanas, and dhātus are completely illuminated.
This treasure of the Buddhavacana has been secured by the strenuous efforts of devoted masters, scholars, translators, patrons, and rulers of Tibet, but it is not only for Tibetans that it has been preserved. That these teachings have endured to the present day is truly cause for gratitude and celebration. May they continue to liberate myriad beings for as long as we draw breath upon Jambudvīpa.

Sarvam Maṅgalam, Jayantu Ho
Refugee Tarthang-pa Kunga Gellek Yeshe Dorje
Head Lama, Tibetan Nyingma Meditation Center
Founder of the Yeshe De Project,
Sarnath International Nyingma Institute,
and the Khen Lop Chö Sum Foundation
Odiyan Copper Mountain Mandala
January 13, 2025, on the day of the Full Moon
Oṃ ye dharmā hetu prabhavā hetuṃ teṣāṃ tathāgato hyavadat teṣāṃ ca yo nirodha evaṃ vādī mahā śramaṇaḥ ye svāhā

*Preface*
*Homage to Buddha, Dharma, and Sangha!*

*Due to immeasurable compassion and wisdom, combined with sentient beings’ meritorious accumulations, Shakyamuni Buddha turned the Wheel of Dharma for the sake of all beings. From these magnanimous turnings, a light was generated, so brilliant that it became the luminescence of 84,000 teachings, given to benefit all beings throughout time and space.*
*For each and every one’s unique mental faculties and dispositions, the Buddhavacana, the sublime words of the Buddha, has been offered to cure all forms of suffering and reveal the precious, abiding, indivisible jewel inherent in us all.*

For over 55 years, Tarthang Rinpoche has supported the production and distribution of millions of sacred Dharma texts, including thousands of copies of historically important versions of the Kangyur, the canonical words of the Buddha as translated into Tibetan. Distributed over the years at the annual Nyingma Monlam Chenmo at Bodhgaya, these sacred texts have been carried away by devoted Buddhist pilgrims. Returning home by bus, train, horse, and yak, book-by-book more than three thousand altars and libraries have been created throughout the Himalayas and Tibet, heralding the largest Buddhist text preservation and distribution in history.

Rinpoche arrived in India in 1958, having followed his revered master Jamyang Khyentse Chokyi Lodro into exile. In 1962, His Holiness Dujdom Rinpoche asked Rinpoche to teach at Sanskrit University in Varanasi. He quickly established one of Tibet’s first printing houses in exile, Dharma Mudranalaya. From these humble origins, in a wooden shack outside the Burmese monastery in Varanasi, Rinpoche set in motion a lifelong commitment to Buddhist text preservation by employing hand-set-type and a hand-operated letterpress.
In 2018, some 60 years later, Rinpoche requested the khenpos and students at the Sarnath International Nyingma Institute (SINI), which he had founded in 2006, to start a close reading of the Kangyur, in preparation for a major research and publication project that became the Kangyur Karchag. How amazing that more than 2,500 years after the Buddha first turned the Wheel of the Dharma in Sarnath, a small group of relatively young scholars living in Sarnath would be asked to summarize those same teachings for the benefit of Dharma students today!
A year later, in 2019, Rinpoche launched the Kangyur Karchag project, with the aim of creating a new *karchag*, a descriptive catalog, of the Kangyur, based on Yeshe De’s 133-volume Yidzin Norbu Kangyur (published by Dharma Publishing in 2012). Many Kangyur karchag catalogs have been produced in Tibet’s long and illustrious history of Buddhist text preservation and transmission. This new Karchag had a special aim: to build on a wide array of historical sources and new approaches to summarize the contents of the sutras.
Before long, through creative, iterative conversations, an expansive vision began to unfold, as Rinpoche provided the editors with additional research questions. For instance, he asked them to look for ways to classify and connect the teachings according to different philosophical schools and to determine which unique words or terms should be associated with a particular teaching.
In early 2019, initially with a small team of lamas, SINI editors traveled throughout India’s exile communities to meet masters of the four major schools of Tibetan Buddhism (Nyingma, Kagyu, Sakya, and Gelug) and seek their blessings, guidance, and support. An audience with Dzongsar Khyentse Rinpoche in April 2019 helped to secure further blessings and confidence. He encouraged us to apply for a Khyentse Foundation grant, which we were generously awarded yearly for the duration of our project.

*Polishing and Protecting a Precious Heirloom for Humanity*
This grand, non-sectarian project aims to provide a more systematic presentation of the scriptural treasures contained within the Kangyur. By summarizing, clarifying, and connecting the streams of knowledge passed down from the Buddha, in the form of a reader-friendly encyclopedia, our mission has been to offer accessibility and to further interest in the general study and contemplation of the words of the Buddha.
The history of the Kangyur, also known as the Tibetan Buddhist Canon, dates to the period immediately following the Parinirvana of the Buddha (circa 500 BCE), a great council was convened to recite and codify the *Buddhavacana,* the words of the Buddha. In successive generations over many centuries, the Buddha’s followers would gather periodically and, initially through oral recitation, transmit and uphold his precious words. The history of the transmission of the Dharma throughout Asia is long and rich. One can appreciate how it manifested in great textual collections, most notably in the production of kangyurs.
During the 8th century in Tibet, one of the most powerful and unique collaborations in the process of the Dharma’s transmission was formed by the diamond trio, Khen Lop Cho Sum–the Abbot Shankaratshita (Khen), the Lotus-Born master Padmasambhava (Lop), and the Tibetan Emperor Trison Detsen (Cho). Through the blessings of the Khen Lop Cho Sum and the compassionate efforts of hundreds of Tibetan lotsawas (translators) and Indian panditas (scholars), a profound and rich translation and commentarial tradition emerged. From then on, great Buddhist masters and practitioners have cultivated, contemplated, translated, and preserved the Buddhavacana into the twenty-first century. As the commentarial tradition (*shastra*) flourished in Tibet, specific practice lineages emerged, reflecting subtle philosophical differences and approaches to the path of realization. Studied faithfully, these lineages provided gateways to awakening and a continuous stream of blessings.
In caring for such a beautiful, heirloom ornament as the Kangyur, one must not only polish the jewels but also give attention to the settings that link and support them. Where possible, this new karchag aims to *clarify* and *connect* both the Indian and Tibetan commentarial traditions to the Kangyur. In this way, we will help support our shared bonds of Dharma and help connect our understanding more directly with the original words of the Buddha.
*A Kangyur Karchag for the Twenty-First Century*
Today, individual sutras, such as the *Heart Sutra*, *Lotus Sutra*, and *Rice Seedling Sutra*, are recited, studied, and, at higher levels, expounded upon in commentaries. However, no general course on the study of the Kangyur currently exists in traditional monastic settings. In fact, not a single text or course book written in Tibetan systematically describes and introduces the entire Kangyur. The Kangyur Karchag addresses this gap by offering students a comprehensive summary of the Kangyur, enabling them to grasp its complete contents, something that even advanced scholars rarely achieve.
To accomplish this, we have summarized the contents of all the Kangyur’s sutras in ten volumes. Two additional volumes include an overview of the tantras, a history of the Buddha’s lifestory, and a compilation of essays on the Kangyur composed by some of today’s most erudite Tibetan scholars from different schools. A final abbreviated reference volume condenses the contents of all ten volumes into one volume and is meant to sit on desks for close and easy access to a detailed overview of the Kangyur.
In the ecumenical spirit of this project, several key considerations have been at the center of our hearts: how to help encourage the study of the Buddha›s teachings, how to make them more accessible, and how to help prepare Kangyur study materials that will lead to the creation of contemporary Buddhist textbooks for schools in the Himalayas, India, and beyond.
In general, for the Dharma to survive, clearly the roots of the Dharma must be nourished through a vast approach to understanding its source: the words of the Buddha. The formation of this encyclopedia will provide a gateway into the study of the Kangyur. The Kangyur encyclopedia project has already proven in its research process to be an excellent opportunity for wide-scale collaboration. It has motivated some of Tibet’s best writers-in-exile to come forward to support this goal. We hope the formation of this encyclopedia will provide a gateway into the study of the Kangyur for the public as well.

*The Basis for This Kangyur Karchag: The 2012 Yeshe De Yidzhin Norbu Kangyur*
The Yeshe De Yidhzin Norbu Kangyur was produced in 2012 by Tarthang Rinpoche and distributed freely to more than 3,300 monasteries, libraries, and learning centers throughout the Himalayas. This version of the Kangyur, based largely on the 18th-century Derge edition and checked against the Pedurma edition produced in 2009, includes several notable additions, including the Pali version of the *Dhammapada Sutra* translated by Gendun Choephel (1905-1951). It also contains the Chinese translation of the *Mahavibhasa* and a collection of 16 karchags spanning the 9th to 20th centuries. The *Yidzhin Norbu Yeshe De Kangyur* consists of 133 Western-style volumes, containing 368 sutras and 773 tantras.
*The Word* “*Karchag*” *(dKar chag)*

When I first encountered the definition of *karchag* as a *descriptive catalog* or *index*, my initial reaction was one of mild interest. A catalog that lists the number of folios and *bampos* in a sutra, the heart of most of the early karchags, seemed tedious and pedantic. What value could such “statistical” information hold that would inspire one to read the Kangyur? However, as I observed the deeper significance of a karchag and witnessed the meticulous process involved in creating one today, my appreciation for its value and its beauty grew.
The earliest use of the word *karchag* dates to the Imperial period (circa 618-842 CE). While some dictionaries, define “dkar chag” as a register, index, catalog, list, or table of contents; the word can also be a synonym for qualities such as sincerity, candidness, morality, or wholesomeness.
According to Professor Padma Gyaltsen (1956-2001), the etymology of “dkar chag” can be traced to the words “dkar”, meaning white color, and “chag”, relating to measurement. He writes that royal messengers marked distances between regions with white soil, a practice called “dkar chag.” Over time, this term evolved to encompass measurements in general. For Buddhist scriptures, “dkar chag” became associated with the precise listing of volumes and verses. Inspired by the meaning of “dkar chag” (a roadside marker that guides travelers), we designed a chart in the shape of a stone plinth.
The scholar Dungkar Lobsang Thinley elaborates that “dkar chag” refers to a clear list, designed to allow for easy navigation through scriptures, books, and documents by categorizing and enumerating them. “Dkar” symbolizes clarity, akin to seeing objects clearly in daylight, while “chag” signifies the separation and organization of categories, much like bundling grass. A different interpretation, offered by Samdhong Rinpoche, suggests that “dkar chag” might have originated from “ka chag”, referring to the alphabetical arrangement of volumes, which then became “dkar chag.” This interpretation emphasizes the alphabetical ordering of texts. Lutsang Dorje Rinchen views “dkar chag” as a system for listing titles of various compositions, likening it to a key that unlocks countless doors, facilitating the search and extraction of writings. While these interpretations vary, they agree that a “dkar chag” is not merely a simple list but an intricate system of ordering and categorization.
*Navigating the Unfamiliar: Making the Project Real*

Work on the new Kangyur Karchag began in the library of the Sarnath International Nyingma Institute, a new campus established by Tarthang Rinpoche in Sarnath to encourage monastics to learn English and explore new avenues for presenting the Dharma today. As we considered the requirements of the project, it quickly became clear that the expertise and efforts of many would be needed to carry out Rinpoche›s vision.

Despite being the daughter of a Tibetan lama, I felt daunted by the Karchag project, given my own relatively limited Tibetan language skills and modest familiarity with the Kangyur. Yet, moved by Rinpoche›s conviction about the project›s urgency and significance, I committed to contributing however I could, even if only to support and celebrate the team›s remarkable work. I bore in mind that as a master of skillful means, Rinpoche always finds a way for everyone to participate in meritorious projects. Building and nurturing a team would be of central importance.

As I reflected on how best to proceed, I drew upon my experience coordinating the restoration of the Svayambhu Stupa, in Kathmandu, Nepal, one of the most sacred places in the Buddhist world with more than 1,500 years of documented history. Managing this challenging project taught me that a shared clarity of purpose is key in guiding teams of skilled experts. Since then, as SINI's founding director, I have tried to create practical bridges that allow people with diverse points of view to work together and produce tangible accomplishments. For these collaborations to succeed, an inspiring vision needs to be communicated, a vision that can be shared and transmitted by all participants.

Needing a way to easily communicate what a *karchag* is to people outside of the Tibetan-speaking world, I asked several Tibetan scholars if the term “encyclopedia” would be an approximate term to use. They said it made sense. Some Western professors remarked that I could also call it a “sutrapedia” (a play on the words “sutra” and “wikipedia”) conveying the sense of an essential summary, or *karchag* in our case. The editors decided to keep the term “encyclopedia,” as the work includes extensive summaries of the tantra section, as well as important essays from senior contemporary scholars on the Kangyur in general. The SINI Karchag, then, was not just about summarizing the sutras; it could be a way to give the world access to the entire Kangyur.

With a growing sense of the project’s potential, we consulted with Professor Samdhong Rinpoche, one of the foremost Tibetan Buddhist scholars alive today, who reviewed the first sample versions of our sutra summaries and provided detailed editorial responses. Thrangu Rinpoche also took a keen interest in our work and was involved in early discussions with the core editorial team about how best to carry out this massive project. Both senior Dharma experts graciously provided us with feedback and suggested enhancements throughout the project.
Due to the size and scope of this project’s mission, we required strong intellectual leadership and excellent organizational skills. We were extremely fortunate to have Khenpo Ju Tenkyong of the Larung Gar Institute serve as the Chief Editor. His years of experience as the editor of the 223-volume Tibetan terminological dictionary and his general knowledge of the Kangyur and Tangyur provided the critical motivational, intellectual, and organizational foundation for this project.
In Sarnath, we established an administrative research team that coordinated with the Central Institute of Higher Tibetan Studies, also in Sarnath, and with the Tibetan Library of Works and Archives in Dharamsala. Both institutions generously offered their access to sources and research assistance. In addition, we relied heavily on the treasuries of knowledge contained in the freely available databases of the Buddhist Digital Resource Center (BDRC), founded by E. Gene Smith, and the inspiring, pioneering work of 84000.
To streamline the complex editorial process, we appointed two highly qualified senior editors from Namdroling: Khenpo Thupten Gyatso and Khenpo Thupten Lobsang. They worked tirelessly during the Covid global pandemic, reading more than 700 Kangyur-related texts over five years, coordinating the contributions of over 50 other contributors, and editing their own submissions numerous times. Additionally, without the determined efforts of Khenpo Tsering Nyima, the precious and complex coordination of this work would not have been possible.
Although each summary varied, we sought to standardize entries so that the reader could understand the essence of the text and readily identify additional resources for further study. In addition to the prominent lamas serving as advisors to this work, we assembled a team of approximately fifty khenpos, geshes, lopons, and researchers mainly based in India, with some team members located in Bhutan, Nepal, and Tibet.
As we undertook this work, I observed firsthand how a new cohort of young researchers, most of them in their thirties and forties, were participating in the most significant project of their lives. The results of their role in this project will undoubtedly unfold over their lives, as they support other major Dharma projects in the future with the skills they have gained.

*Blessings, Feedback, and Support*
Once we had established our workflow (including printing out a thirty-foot-long project timeline kept in SINI’s library), we laid out the materials we were going to summarize and assembled a core team in a dedicated office workspace. We also established the qualifications for the contributing writers, and what the editorial process would be. With these decisions in place, we were ready to begin the division of work. Between 2019 and the start of the Covid pandemic, we held meetings with the most senior lamas in all the traditions to receive their blessings, support, and guidance. In particular, we were very fortunate to have an audience with His Holiness the 14th Dalai Lama to share our main goals. His enthusiastic encouragement, blessings, and letter of support were anchors of hope for the project. His Holiness the 41st Sakya Trichen also kindly conferred blessings and a deep sense of historical continuity on our group. His Holiness Drikung Chetsang Rinpoche offered two of his senior khenpos to work on the *karchag*.

Toward the end of the editorial process, I turned to one of the senior editors and asked what he was working on. He told me that he was adding some notes to the 2,000-page digital notebook he had created while working on the Kangyur Karchag. My eyes filled with tears as I asked him, “Do you mean that in addition to the work you did, you wrote 2,000 pages of notes?” He replied, “Yes, I kept a separate log of all the points and questions I had about our work, in a single Word document.” Such examples of dedication have amazed me throughout this blessed project.

In the summer of 2024, the eleven meticulously researched and edited volumes were proofread in Manali by a team of nine editors. The proofreading process was conducted in four stages to ensure accuracy and quality: initial verification of content; addressing spelling, grammar, and omissions; further refining accuracy and consistency; followed by a final hard copy review to confirm all corrections.
*A Brief Description of the Contents of the Sutra Summaries*
Each summary includes a detailed table of contents, an explanatory key, covering various aspects such as the Tibetan, Chinese, Sanskrit, English, and alternate title information. It provides the classification of the sutra according to the vehicles, the designation of teachings as provisional or definitive, and the identification of which turning of the wheel of Dharma a text belongs to. It also defines structural elements such as chapters, bampos, and pages and discusses methods for determining if the text belongs to the earlier or later translation period.
