It outlines the process of searching for commentaries, exploring the purpose of the text, and provides a concise meaning.
The summary further examines the five perfect conditions for teaching (place, teacher, retinue, time, and teaching itself), known in Tibetan as the *pun sum tshog pa nga*, explains key terms and the concept of impetus in the text, and includes a section on addressing potential objections and refutations. This comprehensive approach ensures a thorough understanding and analysis of the sutras, their context, and their significance within the tradition. Each sutra summary also includes an area of “special notes” with additional information about the sutra available in other versions of the Kangyur, commentaries, or even the editors’ views.
Although this grand project initially met with both encouragement and skepticism from scholars and laymen alike, my strong sense was that it was less about attempting to generate definitive conclusions than about a group of young scholars becoming familiar with the Kangyur, appreciating its contents and directly experiencing the challenges and educational benefits of such a comprehensive project. How very gratifying that this intensive, group-led research process has yielded such positive results in a relatively short time.
Now that we have completed the first edition of the karchag, we hope our work will create further conditions for research and study. Perhaps most importantly, it could help encourage the development of new curricula related to Kangyur studies in both secular and non-secular settings. With these summaries in hand, both monastic and lay students will have, for the first time, an opportunity to get a general overview of the Buddha’s teaching. By using this encyclopedia, students will be able to learn about the main divisions of the Kangyur, study summaries of each sutra, learn about key commentaries on the sutras, and so much more. We pray that in reading these summaries, students will be inspired to read the original sutras and bring the teachings alive in everyday life.

*Supporting Accessibility*
In the West, Bible study groups and similar religious reading groups have supported the preservation and transmission of scripture. In creating this karchag, we hope something similar may take shape in the wake of our work. Our intention has been to compose accessible and inspiring summaries of the sutras that will pave the way for an increase in not only monastic but also lay studies of the Dharma. For us, removing the barriers to studying the words of the Buddha is a gesture of love and respect for all of humanity. Now that the Tibetan version has been completed, we hope that others will be inspired to join us in translating the summaries into other important widely spoken languages such as English, Chinese, and Hindi.
As we come to appreciate what this catalog represents for both Buddhist scholars and the lay community, we believe we have provided the basis for Kangyur studies in both monasteries and lay communities. The plans for the next steps of this project are already unfolding. In addition to the original compositions of this encyclopedia, several of SINI’s senior khenpos who are enrolled in the English for Dharma Purposes program have been working with PhD candidates at Western universities to translate the summaries into English. Employing all the translation and research tools available, these dedicated teams of translators are already producing impressive results.

As our attention spans become shorter and our lives more busy, the need to make the Dharma more accessible steadily grows. To reach the next generation, we need to provide materials that are not only inspiring but also use relatable language. Language that is archaic or overly academic may work well for the scholar, but it is unlikely to be appreciated by or of much use to a lay audience. Historically, karchags and most of the commentaries on the Kangyur were written by and for scholars and monastics. We believe that this newly created, more accessible karchag marks an important advance in extending the blessings of the Buddha’s speech and widening the path for its followers.

*Reflections and Appreciation*
In reflecting on this project, I reviewed some of Tarthang Rinpoche’s earlier writings, where I found this beautiful passage, drawn from the Yidzhin Norbu’s concluding essay:

Preserved through the centuries as manuscripts written on palm leaves, later as prints made from wood blocks, the high value of the texts themselves is apparent and palpable, yet the efforts and sacrifices, as well as the long-range intentions behind the Kangyur, remain largely invisible. The invisibility could be considered the Bodhisattva’s signature. Only when we shift our perspective can we catch a glimpse of their profound purposes, which transcend the identity-centered orientation of the samsaric mind and do not explicitly appear in the pages of our histories. If we look carefully, however, we can discover an exceptional array of circumstances and a long line of extraordinary masters who appeared over many centuries to ensure the longevity of the light of knowledge these texts radiate.
Reflecting on these circumstances and imagining the history in some detail creates the impression of powerful forces at work that transcend the karma of ordinary sentient beings. Buddhist texts describe the enormous force of the Bodhisattva’s vow and aspirations that can hold a higher purpose, the momentum of accumulated goodness that clears the way for remarkable action, and the skillful methods and inner strength that guarantee the results. These qualities emerge on the higher reaches of the Bodhisattva path, the qualities required to keep the light shining in the darkness of samsara.

A project of this magnitude rests on countless contributions, both seen and unseen. On behalf of our editorial team and contributors, I extend my deepest gratitude to all the precious lamas whose blessings and guidance illuminated our path. I am also deeply grateful to my parents, whose encouragement and love have inspired me to continually deepen my potential and not shy away from tasks that seem impossible. My heartfelt thanks go out to the friends and donors of the Sarnath International Nyingma Institute. They not only entrusted us with this sacred task but also celebrated each milestone. Most of the benefactors of this project come from varied cultural backgrounds and do not speak Tibetan. They are, perhaps, unlikely to directly benefit from reading this publication. However, their support exemplifies the Bodhisattva›s selfless generosity, an invisible thread of wisdom and compassion binding us together.

As always, we ask for forgiveness for any mistakes made in this project and welcome corrections for future reprints and the forthcoming online version. May the guardians of the Dharma uphold their sacred vows, protect these treasures, and secure the streams of transmission!

*Dedication*
Here, in Sarnath, where the Buddha first turned the Wheel of Dharma, where the Ganges has flowed for millennia from the snow-capped mountains of Tibet to the Bay of Bengal, our work is dedicated to everlasting peace in the world and the longevity of our precious lineage holders.

Sarvam Maṅgalam, Jayantu Ho

Tsering Palmo Gellek
SINI Director

བཅོམ་ལྡན་འདས་དེ་བཞིན་གཤེགས་པ་དགྲ་བཅོམ་པ་ཡང་དག་པར་རྫོགས་པའི་སངས་རྒྱས་
ཤཱཀྱ་ཐུབ་པ་ལ་ཕྱག་འཚལ་ལོ་མཆོད་དོ་སྐྱབས་སུ་མཆིའོ།།

An Overview of the Contents and Significance of the Kangyur Karchag

*General Introduction*

As Buddhist texts climbed their way from South and East Asia to the Roof of the World nearly 1,400 years ago, they inspired one of the greatest literary achievements in human history. Over 5,000 texts from various Asian languages, consisting of over 230,000 folios, 300 volumes, and 73 million words, began to be translated into literary Tibetan. After 800 years, the work of more than 700 translators and scholars was brought together in the Tibetan Buddhist Canon, consisting of the Kangyur (*bka’ ‘gyur*), Words of the Buddha, and Tengyur (*bstan ‘gyur*), Scholastic Treatises.

The canon’s immensity inspires awe and reverence, yet creates a significant barrier to entry. How can an interested reader navigate this massive collection? Where would one even begin? As early as the ninth century these concerns began to be addressed, with translated texts being organized into lists under various doctrinal criteria. These catalogs, known by the Tibetan term *karchag* (*dkar chag*), soon became an indispensable companion to the canon.

Efforts to compile, print, and study the entire canon increased in the ensuing centuries, with more and more *karchag*s being produced, their contents expanded. Two particularly significant developments were the cataloging efforts of the Kadampa master Chomden Reldri (1227–1305) and his disciples, as well as Buton Rinchen Drub (1290–1364), whose *karchag*s were foundational in determining both the content and structure of the Kangyur and Tengyur. By the time the famous *Derge Kangyur* was printed in 1733, the *karchag* had expanded well beyond a mere list of texts, to include lengthy descriptions of global and local Buddhist history, an account of the physical production of that edition of the Kangyur, and extensive discussions on how to understand the texts. Such discussions center around the different ways of classifying the texts, to better understand them in their proper historical and doctrinal context.

The present *Kangyur Karchag* project, restricted to texts ascribed to the Buddha, stands firmly within this 1,400-year-old *karchag* tradition, giving particular emphasis to the task of classifying texts. The key difference is that rather than discuss different classifications through which the entire collection can be accessed, the *Kangyur Karchag* applies a more expansive list of classifications directly to each text. It then proceeds to analyze and concisely summarize the content. Thus, the *Kangyur Karchag* provides an encyclopedic catalog of the contents of the Kangyur and is therefore referred to in English as the “Encyclopedia Project.” For readers familiar with Tibetan literature, an apt comparison, albeit much less detailed, would be the 16th-century work of Pekar Zangpo called *The General Sūtra Classification* (*mdo sde spyi’i rnam bzhag*).
The *Kangyur Karchag* is divided into two parts: a table consisting of classificatory and bibliographic information for each text and a concise summary and analysis of its contents. The editors are primarily working from the Yidzhin Norbu Edition (2012) of the *Yeshe De Kangyur*—the so-called “Nyingma Edition” published by Dharma Publishing in 1983—but also reference the Derge and Pedurma editions in the table, and occasionally draw attention to variants in other editions in the appendix section discussed in part two below.

A Brief Overview of Part One

Translated into English, Part One appears as follows:
| Tibetan Title<br>Sanskrit Title in Tibetan Script | | | | | |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Sanskrit | [Title in Sanskrit] | | | | |
| Chinese | | [Title in Mandarin Chinese] | | | |
| English | | [Title in English] | | | |
| Derge Kangyur Section | | [#] | Turning of the Wheel | | [1st, 2nd, 3rd] |
| Yeshe De Kangyur Section | | [#] | Vehicle | | [Small, Great, Vajra] |
| Alternate Titles | | | Provisional / Definitive | | |
| Chapters | | [Chapter: Yeshe De Kangyur] | Basket | | [Sūtra, Vinaya, Abhidharma] |
| Bam po | | [#] | Earlier / Later Translation | | |
| Pages | | [#] | Yeshe De Kangyur Volume Number | | [#] |
| Commentaries | | [#] | Pedurma Kangyur Volume Number | | [#] |
| Translator’s Homage | | | | | |
| | | | | | |

The Sanskrit titles are extracted from the opening line of each text. Each text begins with: “In Sanskrit (*rgya gar skad du*) …” followed by the Sanskrit title transliterated into Tibetan script. The following line of each text begins with “In Tibetan (*bod skad du*) …” followed by the Tibetan title, which is usually a translation of the Sanskrit title. Alternate titles often appear throughout a given text, in other *karchag*s, or in other editions of the Kangyur; these are listed in the appendix of each entry in Part Two (see below). The Chinese titles are translations of the Tibetan titles into Mandarin Chinese by bilingual Tibetan scholars, while the English titles have been graciously provided by the 84000 Foundation.
The Turning of the Wheel classification denotes three successive stages during which the Buddha taught the Dharma, as summarized in the seventh chapter of the *Unraveling the Intent Sūtra* (*saṃdhinirmocanasūtra*): (1) the Four Noble Truths taught at the Deer Park in Vārāṇasī; (2) the doctrine of emptiness (śūnyatā), often placed at the Vulture Peak in Rājagṛha; (3) and what the sūtra refers to as the “exceedingly marvelous third wheel endowed with spectacular discernment,” understood here to refer to the Mind-Only (*cittamātra*) and Buddha-Nature (*tathāgatagarbha*) teachings.

Bear in mind that this classification, as well as those described below, have been applied in varied ways throughout Buddhist history. For an alternate interpretation of the Turning of the Wheel category in the Tibetan tradition, see Padma dkar po’s (1527–1592) *Clarifying Doubts about the Three Turnings of the Wheel* (*chos ’khor rim pa gsum gi dogs gcod*). The descriptions given here reflect both the *Kangyur Karchag* editors’ extensive training within the living tradition and the wide range of sources from which they draw. Additional explanations, justifications, and external references describing their use of each classification can be found in the Tibetan introduction, as well as in the appendix section of each entry in Part Two. Frequently consulted sources include the two extant imperial catalogues (*Lhan dkar ma* and *’Phang thang ma*), the fourth chapter of Buton Rinchen Drub’s (1290–1364) *Dharma History* (*bu ston chos ‘byung*), Zhamar Chokyi Drakpa’s (1453–1524) *General Sūtra Classification* (*mdo sde spyi’i rnam gzhag*), Pekar Zangpo’s 16th-century *General Sūtra Classification* (*mdo sde spyi’i rnam bzhag*), and the *Derge Kangyur Karchag*.

“Vehicle” refers to the well-known Three Vehicles (*triyāna*): the Śrāvakayāna (Listener’s Vehicle) the Mahāyāna (Great Vehicle), and Vajrayāna (Diamond Vehicle). The *Kangyur Karchag* limits its analysis of individual texts to only the first two vehicles, the Śrāvakayāna and Mahāyāna. The precise differences between the two are often enumerated as seven-fold, described in detailed commentarial works such as the eleventh chapter of the *Ornament of the Mahāyāna Sūtras* (*mahāyānasūtrālaṃkāra*) and the tenth chapter of the *Compendium of the Mahāyāna* (*mahāyānasaṃgraha*). Here the Śrāvakayāna includes the First Turning of the Wheel described above, while the Mahāyāna includes both the Second and Third.
The “Provisional/Definitive” distinction divides texts into two groups: (1) the provisional that require further elucidation (*neyārtha*), because their teachings were intended for a certain situation or audience; and (2) the definitive (*nītārtha*), because their teachings are direct and universal. These two classifications are often understood alongside the Two Truths distinction, in which the Buddha’s teachings and reality itself can be understood on two corresponding levels: (1) the conventional (*saṃvṛtti*) and (2) the ultimate (*paramārtha*). Here texts belonging to the Mahāyāna are typically denoted as definitive, while those belonging to the Śrāvakayāna are denoted as provisional. Yet the distinction is ultimately drawn by the project editors based on their analysis of the teachings in a given text. Some of the sūtras associated with the Mind-Only tradition, for example, are designated as Provisional.
“Basket” refers to the Three Baskets (*tripiṭaka*) into which the Buddha’s disciples divided his teachings immediately following his parinirvāṇa at the First Council. These are (1) the Sūtra Basket (general discourses of the Buddha), (2) the Vinaya Basket (monastic regulations along with histories of the early Buddhist saṅgha), and (3) the Abhidharma (systematic elucidations of topics in the sūtras, attributed to the Buddha or his senior disciples). In Tibet, this three-fold division is often discussed in terms of three antidotes (*pratipakṣa*) these collections provide—one antidote per basket—to counteract three common defilements. Pekar Zangpo, for example, presents them as antidotes for the Three Poisons (anger, desire, and ignorance, respectively) while Buton says they are antidotes for doubt, the extremes of hedonism and asceticism, and the view of the self. In the *Kangyur Karchag*, the category of Abhidharma draws from the tenth chapter of the *Unraveling the Intent Sūtra* (*saṃdhinirmocanasūtra*), which defines Abhidharma through an elaborate description of eleven topics. In their introduction, the *Kangyur Karchag* editors argue that the teachings on the Four Noble Truths and the doctrine of emptiness are best situated within these topics and thus designate certain texts generally referred to as Sūtra (such as the *Turning of the Wheel Sūtra* (*dharmacakrasūtra*), the *Questions of the Brāhmaṇī Śrīmatī Sūtra* (ś*rīmatībrāhmaṇīparipṛcchā**sūtra*) and the Perfection of Wisdom (*prajñāparamitā*) Sūtras) as Abhidharma. In most other *karchag*s, Abhidharma is a category only used in the Tengyur.
“Bam po” is an indigenous Tibetan unit of textual measurement denoting the number of syllables in a text. A common definition is 300 śloka-s, with a śloka denoting 32 syllables in the case of prose, and four lines of varying (but often eight-syllable) length in the case of verse. However, the term *bam po* seems to have been used in different ways throughout Tibetan translation history, and is an issue that requires further investigation. The term appears frequently in texts translated into Tibetan, where the first *bam po* and so on are successively indicated. These are then tallied up and given in the imperial catalogs. During the two major translation periods (discussed below), the *bam po* seems to have served both to inhibit alterations to the original text and as a unit for calculating the amount of compensation paid to each scribe. Here, the *bam po* number is listed as it appears in a given text or as can be gathered from the imperial catalogs.

“Earlier/Later Translation” refers to the two periods when large-scale translation projects were carried out in Tibet. The first Tibetan translator of Buddhist scriptures is said to be Tonmi Sambhota, the royal minister credited with developing the Tibetan script during the reign of emperor Songsten Gampo (r. 617–c.650 C.E.). Large-scale, imperially sponsored translation began in earnest five generations later, under the emperor Trisong Detsen (r. 765–c.800), the period typically denoted as the Early Translation Period, which continued until the fall of the Tibetan empire in 842. Thus texts attributed to translators from this period as mentioned in the translator’s colophon (discussed in Part Two below) or other sources such as the imperial catalogs and early Tibetan historiographical works when there is no colophon, are designated as Earlier. At some point in the mid- to late-tenth century, a new period of translation took shape, known as the Later Translation Period. The precise moment when this period began is a topic of debate among Tibetan historians; a commonly identified moment is when Rinchen Zangpo (958–1055) returned to Western Tibet from his language training in India around 973 to begin translating Buddhist texts anew. For the *Kangyur Karchag*, however, the distinction is quite straightforward, as the Later Translation Period simply refers to texts translated by translators from the mid- to late-tenth century onwards.
“Commentaries” refer primarily to texts in the Tengyur that explain in further detail a corresponding text in the Kangyur. These are primarily translations of Indian scholastic works and have been gathered through an analysis of the Sūtra Commentaries section of the Tengyur. Yet, additional commentaries produced in Tibet, mentioned in the imperial catalogues and/or early Tibetan historiographical works but largely unknown to modern Kangyur Studies, are also mentioned here. See, for example, *The Memorandum on the Rice Seedling* composed by the Dharma King Trisong Detsen (*chos rgyal khri srong lde btsan gyis mdzad pa’i sa lu ljang pa’i brjed byang*) listed in the entry for the *Rice Seedling Sūtra* (śālistambhasūtra).
The “Translator’s Homage” typically appears at the beginning of each sūtra, with a different homage given for each of the Three Baskets. A text from the Sūtra Basket will typically begin with the phrase, “Homage to all buddhas and bodhisattvas!” The Vinaya Basket will have, “Homage to the Omniscient One!” or “Homage to the Three Jewels!” The Abhidharma Basket will have, “Homage to the youthful Mañjuśrī!”

*A Brief Overview of Part Two*

Although the *Kangyur Karchag* is more of an encyclopedia than a catalog or commentary proper, its comparatively brief analyses and summaries of each text combine two traditional exegetical schemes. The first is more common to the Nyingma tradition of Tibetan Buddhism, while the second holds an important position in both Indian and Tibetan scholastic traditions: (1) The Five Perfections (*phun sum tshogs pa lnga*) consisting of the Perfect Teacher, Time, Place, Retinue, and Teaching; (2) The Five Means of Explanation (*bshad thabs yan lag lnga*) as laid out in the renowned *Principles of Exegesis* (*vyākhyāyukti*) attributed to the fourth- or fifth-century Indian scholar Vasubandhu, consisting of the Purpose, Concise Meaning, Meaning of Key Terms, Sequence, and Responses to Objections (although these terms are applied here in a slightly different manner than prescribed by Vasubandhu). Following a unique combination of these two exegetical schemes, the *Kangyur Karchag* also provides the Translator’s Colophon and an Appendix.

In the *Kangyur Karchag*, the Five Means of Explanation serve as the overriding exegetical structure, while the Five Perfections serve as a gloss to the Concise Meaning section. Translated into English, the structure looks like this:

| 1. Purpose (dgos pa; prayojana)<br>a. Temporal (gnas skabs) Purpose / Ultimate (mthar) Purpose<br>2. Concise Meaning (bsdus pa’i don; piṇḍārtha) Five Perfections 2.1. Perfect Teacher (*ston pa*)<br>2.2. Perfect Retinue (’*khor ba*)<br>2.3. Perfect Place (*gnas*)<br>2.4. Perfect Time (*dus*)<br>2.5. Perfect Teaching (*chos*) (summarized into subsections) 3. Meaning of Key Terms (tshig gi don; padārtha) 4. Sequence (mtshams sbyar ba; anusaṃdhi) 5. Responses to Objections (brgal lan; codyaparihāra)<br>6. Translator’s Colophon<br>7. Appendix |
| --- |
| |

In the *Kangyur Karchag*, the “Purpose” is two-fold: temporal (*gnas skabs*) and ultimate (*mthar*), often appearing in commentarial works in the Tengyur as the purpose (*dgos pa*) and purpose of the purpose (*dgos pa’i dgos pa*). These refer to the immediate benefits that can be gleaned from a given text (such as an understanding of a particular doctrine or the effects of chanting a particular mantra), followed by how these benefits can culminate in liberation from saṃsāra. This section often concludes with a brief quotation from the sūtra to express these benefits.
The “Concise Meaning” consists entirely of the Five Perfections. With the exception of the fifth of the Five Perfections, these are usually straightforward statements (often extracted directly from the sūtra itself) declaring who taught the sūtra, when and where they taught it, and in the presence of whom it was taught. The most repetitive category here is that of Time, which is often simply glossed with a quotation of the opening phrase of the sūtra: “Thus have I heard at one time.” However, note that this phrase is of utmost significance by indicating that the text is the word of the Buddha, which harkens back to when the Buddha’s teachings were recalled and recited at the First Council. This phrase also highlights the aural and mnemonic nature of these texts, which were transmitted orally for around 500 years before being committed to writing. Hearing and remembering what one heard was thus the sole means of transmitting the Buddha’s word for the early Buddhist community. When this statement is missing, the editors make a note of it and often discuss in the appendix whether it should really be considered the Buddha’s word.
Occasionally, the time of the sūtra is also a matter of debate, as in the case of the *Turning of the Wheel Sūtra* (*dharmacakrasūtra*), or the retinue and/or teacher is unclear, as in the *Questions on Selflessness Sūtra* (*nairātmaparipṛcch**āsūtra*) and the *Sūtra on the Three Heaps* (*triskandhakasūtra*), in which case these issues are dealt with in detail within the Five Perfections section or in the appendix. From the perspective of Kangyur Studies, such instances are of immense value for understanding the reception of Buddhist scriptures in Tibet, as the editors draw attention to previously unknown debates on the issues by Tibetan scholars.
The “Perfect Teaching” consists of a concise summary of the text, often broken up by various sub-headings. Choosing which sections merit inclusion here is ultimately a decision made by the editor, based on their personal training and study of the sūtra, but often takes into consideration how previous scholars have parsed the text, the sources for which being discussed in the appendix. This section contributes significant advances toward making the Kangyur accessible to a lay audience, providing a concise overview of a particular text in clear literary Tibetan.
In Vasubandhu’s *Principles of Exegesis*, the “Meaning of Key Terms” is the lengthiest of the five categories, covering a wide range of semantic issues from polysemy to etymology. When the *Kangyur Karchag* project began, this section was similarly expansive yet soon began to exceed the scope and aims of the project. Currently, this section only explains why each text was given its particular title. Yet, sometimes difficult terms remain glossed in the appendix. See, for example, the appendix of the *Wisdom at the Time of Death Sūtra* (*atyayajñānasūtra*).
“Sequence” is conversely the briefest category of Vasubandhu’s *Principles of Exegesis*, justifying how the various topics of a given text are ordered. The *Kangyur Karchag* editors employ this term in an idiosyncratic way, mirroring one of the categories often found in Pekar Zangpo’s *The General Sūtra Classification*, that of the Petitioner (*zhu ba po*). Thus, here the Sequence section consists of a brief statement about who asked the initial question or made the initial request that resulted in the teacher of that particular text giving a teaching.
“Responses to Objections” refers here both to points of debate within the narrative of a given text and doctrinal and historical issues that have become points of debate among scholars within the Indian and Tibetan scholastic traditions. This is another place where the *Kangyur Karchag* editors draw attention to important issues in Tibet’s reception of Buddhist scripture. A good example here is the Responses to Objections section in the *Noble Deeds of Sumāgadhā* (*sumāghadhāvadāna*), which draws attention to how a particular prophecy attributed to the Buddha Kāśyapa (the Buddha immediately prior to the most recent Buddha, Śākyamuni) has been employed in debates about when the early Buddhist saṅgha in India split into the eighteen early Buddhist schools.
The “Translator’s Colophon” is not an exegetical category, but a translation of what are often the final lines of each text: a record of the names of the Tibetan translators (*lo tsa ba*) and Indian scholars (*paṇdita*) who worked on producing this translation. The Translator’s Colophon also gives the names of the revisers (*zhu chen*) who were tasked with overseeing the final translation and implementing a series of language revisions that took place in the latter part of the Early Translation Period. The translator’s colophon is thus a crucial addition to this project, as it serves to justify the placement of the text in a particular translation period. This record also speaks to the method by which these texts were translated, that is, through a collaboration of experts in both the source and target languages, and enables a correlation between biographies of particular Tibetan masters with the translation projects.
The “Appendix” varies significantly for each text, but typically contains at least a list of the extant commentaries and alternate titles of a given text, as already described above. The Appendix frequently includes information about the sources employed in the composition of an entry, especially when determining the translation period of a particular text if the colophon is missing, or discussing other issues related to its authenticity. Occasionally it touches on issues of “sequence” as used in Vasubandhu’s *Principles of Exegesis* mentioned above.

*Closing Remarks*

The editors of the *Kagyur Karchag* have undertaken a monumental task of textual analysis and organization, providing scholars and practitioners alike with a comprehensive guide to navigating the vast corpus of transmitted Buddhist teachings. Their meticulous work demonstrates a commendable commitment to accuracy and fidelity to the original sources, while simultaneously making these treasures of Buddhist transmission more accessible to a wider audience. This achievement represents a significant contribution to the field of Tibetan Buddhist studies and the ongoing efforts to preserve and understand the rich textual traditions of Buddhism.
Efforts have already begun to translate the *Kangyur Karchag* into English, with plans to translate it into Chinese and Hindi. The English translation is an extension of the Sarnath International Nyingma Institute’s Translation Program, which trains Buddhist monastics and equips them with the linguistic and research skills needed to engage in the vital work of translating Buddhist texts from Classical Tibetan and Sanskrit into English. As Buddhist texts make their descent from the Roof of the World down to the rest of the world, how fitting that representatives of the tradition of Tibetan saints and scholars, who dedicated their lives centuries ago to the art of translation, take up the mantle once again? As editors of the *Kangyur Karchag*, they have had the precious opportunity to play a direct role in the translation of their own contributions to the Karchag. It has been a privilege for me as a native speaker of English, the new target language for the transmission of Buddhist scriptures, to witness firsthand the early days of the newest “translation period” and work alongside the most recent members of the long lineage of Tibetan savants dedicated to the transmission of Buddhist teachings.

Tanner McAlister, Ph.D. Candidate, U.C. Berkeley
With contributions from Alexander von Rospatt, Catherine and William L. Magistretti Distinguished Professor of Buddhist and South Asian Studies, U.C. Berkeley
